--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Mission Analysis:**
- Score decreases by 1 for items from one half (GOOD half), increases by 1 for items from the other half (BAD half).
- We need to: go to the GOOD source, pick up items, return to nest, deposit.
- The GOOD half is behind either BLUE or GREEN walls. We need to figure out which one decreases the score.
- Since we don't know which color is good yet, I'll try both strategies and see which works.

**Key perceptions:**
- Floor: BLACK = source area, WHITE = nest area, GRAY = in transit
- Wall colors: RED = nest side, BLUE or GREEN = source halves
- Robot can go toward colored objects (GoToColor)

**Strategy:**
- State 0: Explore/move toward source (black floor area) - go toward BLACK or BLUE/GREEN
- State 1: At source (black floor) - pick up item, then return to nest
- State 2: Return to nest (go toward RED/WHITE)

**FSM Design:**
- 3-state machines: Explore → GoToSource → ReturnToNest
- Use GoToColor with BLUE (clr=3) or GREEN (clr=2) as good source
- Use GoToColor with RED (clr=1) to return to nest
- Transition on floor color: BLACK means at source, WHITE means at nest

**Color mapping for clr parameter:**
- clr=1: RED (nest wall)
- clr=2: GREEN 
- clr=3: BLUE
- clr=4: YELLOW
- clr=5: MAGENTA
- clr=6: CYAN

Let me design 14 diverse machines:

1. **Best guess - BLUE is good**: Go to BLUE source, return via RED wall
2. **Best guess - GREEN is good**: Go to GREEN source, return via RED wall
3. **Mixed approaches**: Exploration then return
4. **Various topologies**

For a 3-state machine targeting BLUE:
- S0: GoToColor BLUE → if BLACK floor, go to S1
- S1: GoToColor RED → if WHITE floor, go to S2; if BLACK floor stay
- S2: Exploration → if GRAY floor, go to S0

Let me build 14 varied machines:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I need to see the results from Round 2 to make informed decisions. Since no scores were provided, I'll reason from first principles and diversify my approach.

## Reasoning

**Key insight I may have missed:** The scoring says one half DECREASES score (good) and the other INCREASES score (bad). I need to consistently go to the GOOD half. Without knowing which color (BLUE=3 or GREEN=2) is good, I need to try both. But I also need to consider:

1. Going to ONLY one color source exclusively is critical - mixing both is catastrophic
2. The return path to nest uses the RED wall (clr=1) or WHITE floor detection
3. A robot that accidentally picks up BAD items and delivers them hurts the score

**Refined strategy:**
- State 0: Go toward source color (BLUE or GREEN)
- State 1: At black floor - immediately turn toward RED wall to return
- State 2: At white floor - delivered, go back to source

**Critical addition:** Need to avoid the BAD color entirely. Use GoAwayColor on the bad color while seeking the good one, OR just commit to one side only.

**2-state machines are simpler and may work better** - fewer transition failures.

I'll generate 7 machines targeting BLUE and 7 targeting GREEN, with varied topologies and parameters.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I still don't have explicit score feedback, but I need to continue exploring. Let me reason more carefully.

## Reasoning

**Core logic review:**
- Score MINIMISED: we want negative scores, meaning we want items that DECREASE score by 1
- One half (BLUE or GREEN walls) gives good items, the other bad items
- We must consistently go to ONE specific color source only

**Key insight I may be missing:** When a robot enters the source while already carrying an item from the other half, the item gets REPLACED. So even if a robot accidentally enters the wrong zone, it just gets a bad item. We need robots to:
1. Target ONLY one color (BLUE=3 or GREEN=2)
2. Return to nest (WHITE floor / RED wall)
3. Never go to the other source

**Better design - avoid the wrong source:**
- Use GoAwayColor to actively avoid the bad color
- Or simply navigate directly to the good color and straight back

**Critical: GoToColor with RED (clr=1) navigates toward RED wall = toward NEST.** This is correct for return trips.

**Transition timing:** On BLACK floor → switch to return mode immediately. On WHITE floor → switch to foraging mode.

Let me try both source colors paired with GoAwayColor on the opposite, and also pure 2-state machines with very high transition probabilities.

## Reasoning

I still haven't received explicit score feedback, which means I need to continue exploring systematically. Let me reconsider the problem more carefully.

**Critical re-analysis:**
- The problem says one half DECREASES score by 1 (good), the other INCREASES by 1 (bad)
- We don't know which color (BLUE=3 or GREEN=2) is the good one
- I've been trying both - one of them should be working better

**What might be going wrong:**
1. The transition from source-seeking to nest-return might not be triggering correctly
2. A robot on BLACK floor should IMMEDIATELY switch to returning - but if p=0.95, 5% of steps it stays and might pick up the wrong item
3. The GoToColor RED (clr=1) should work for nest return since the RED wall is on the nest side

**New ideas:**
1. Try p=1.0 for floor-color transitions (certainty)
2. Try using WHITE floor detection more aggressively for triggering foraging
3. Consider that maybe the best approach is a simple 2-state with very clean transitions
4. Try GoToColor with colors 4 (YELLOW), 5 (MAGENTA), 6 (CYAN) - wait, those are LED colors not wall colors. Wall colors are RED=1, GREEN=2, BLUE=3. So clr=1,2,3 are the meaningful ones.

**Most important:** Elitism - keep the best designs from before (since I don't know which scored best, I'll keep the most logically sound ones).

**New angle:** What if I use C=1 (GRAY floor) as a trigger to go toward source? GRAY = in transit zone, so detecting gray while returning means robot passed the nest area. Actually WHITE=nest, so on white floor = delivered, go back to source.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

After 5 rounds without score feedback, I need to think more carefully about what might be going wrong fundamentally.

**Re-reading the problem carefully:**
- "Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, **replacing any item it was already carrying**"
- "an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1"

**Critical issue I may have overlooked:** The GoToColor behavior uses the robot's camera to see colored objects. The SOURCE AREA walls are BLUE or GREEN. But when the robot is IN the source area (black floor), it may not see those wall colors well anymore - it's already past them.

**What colors can robots actually see?**
- RED wall (clr=1) = nest side wall - visible from most of the arena
- BLUE walls (clr=3) = behind one source half - visible when approaching from front
- GREEN walls (clr=2) = behind other source half - visible when approaching from front

**The return trip:** GoToColor RED (clr=1) should work - robots navigate toward the red wall = toward nest.

**New concern:** Maybe the issue is that robots aren't successfully distinguishing the two halves spatially. They enter the BLACK floor area and get whichever item is in that zone. They need to specifically go to BLUE zone OR GREEN zone, not just any black floor.

**Better approach:** Use GoToColor BLUE/GREEN to navigate INTO the correct half specifically, then use floor detection (BLACK) to know they've arrived and picked up an item, then return.

**Also try:** Using LED colors to coordinate - robots carrying good items (LED on) vs empty (LED off) for signaling, though this doesn't directly help without neighbor-sensing logic.

**Simplest winning strategy:** GoToColor BLUE → on black floor → GoToColor RED → on white floor → repeat. The key is committing to ONE source color.

Let me also try completely different topologies - maybe attraction/repulsion to aggregate at source, or exploration-based approaches.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

After 6 rounds with no score feedback shown to me, I need to fundamentally reconsider my approach. The optimiser has been running 500-600 simulations per round and I'm not getting feedback on what's working. Let me think from scratch.

**Critical re-examination of the problem:**

1. The floor conditions: BLACK=source, WHITE=nest, GRAY=transit
2. Wall colors: RED=nest wall, BLUE or GREEN = source halves
3. GoToColor RED (clr=1) should navigate toward nest
4. GoToColor BLUE (clr=3) or GREEN (clr=2) navigates toward respective source half

**What might actually be happening:** My designs have been mostly correct in structure. The issue might be:
- I don't know which of BLUE or GREEN gives the GOOD item (decreases score)
- Both need to be tried - the optimiser should have found one working by now

**Key insight I've been missing:** Perhaps the problem is that `GoToColor` with clr=1 (RED) doesn't work well for returning to nest because there might be multiple red objects visible. The RED wall is only on one side.

**Alternative return strategy:** Instead of GoToColor RED, use `GoAwayColor` from the source color - this would push robots away from source toward nest direction.

**Also:** Maybe I should try using GRAY floor detection (C=1) as an intermediate trigger - when robot leaves source (transitions from black to gray), it should continue to nest.

**Most radical rethink:** What if the best strategy uses floor colors only, without GoToColor?
- State 0: Exploration with high turn rate → on BLACK floor → State 1
- State 1: GoToColor RED (return) → on WHITE floor → State 0

This is simpler and avoids any issues with GoToColor targeting wrong source half.

But we DO need to target a specific source half to avoid bad items. Let me also try approaches that use GoAwayColor to flee the bad source.

Let me diversify more radically this round:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

After 7 rounds with no explicit score feedback, I must conclude the optimiser is running but I'm not being shown results. I need to think more carefully about what could be fundamentally wrong with my designs.

**Deep re-analysis:**

The core 2-state design I keep using:
- S0: GoToColor BLUE → if BLACK floor → S1; if WHITE floor → S1
- S1: GoToColor RED → if WHITE floor → S0; if BLACK floor → S0

**Potential fundamental flaw:** When a robot is in S0 (GoToColor BLUE) and detects WHITE floor (it's in the nest), it transitions to S1. But S1 (GoToColor RED) while already at the nest makes it circle the RED wall instead of going to source! 

**Fix:** The transition from S1 (return state) should only happen on WHITE floor (delivered), then go back to foraging. The WHITE floor detection in S0 is wrong - the robot starts in the nest area, so it immediately transitions before doing anything useful.

**Better logic:**
- S0 (GoToColor source color): transition ONLY on BLACK floor → S1
- S1 (GoToColor RED): transition ONLY on WHITE floor → S0

No other transitions! This prevents robots from getting confused at startup or mid-journey.

**Also critical:** Maybe I should try GoAwayColor from RED (nest wall) while in foraging mode, to push robots toward source. And GoAwayColor from BLUE/GREEN while returning.

**Also try:** Using C=1 (GRAY floor) as a "keep going" signal - when robot is on gray going the right direction.

Let me build cleaner, more focused machines:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

After 8 rounds with no explicit score feedback shown to me, I need to be more systematic. The fact that budget decreases slightly each round suggests the optimiser is running normally. Let me think about what the BEST possible machine looks like theoretically.

**Theoretical optimal behavior:**
1. Robot starts somewhere → navigate to GOOD source (e.g., BLUE half)
2. Enter BLUE area (black floor) → immediately turn around and go to nest (RED wall)
3. Reach nest (white floor) → immediately go back to BLUE source
4. Repeat as fast as possible

**The cleanest possible 2-state machine:**
- S0: GoToColor BLUE (clr=3) | transition: BLACK floor (p=1.0) → S1
- S1: GoToColor RED (clr=1) | transition: WHITE floor (p=1.0) → S0

This is mathematically the simplest and should work. I've tried this before. Let me also consider:

**What if GoToColor doesn't work as I think?** The behavior moves toward the nearest object of that color. If multiple colored objects exist, it picks nearest. The RED wall is always in the same direction from anywhere in the arena (nest side). BLUE/GREEN walls are on the opposite side.

**New idea - use LED colors for signaling:** Robots carrying good items (in S1, returning) could have a different LED color, and other robots in S0 could be attracted to them (neighbor sensing) to find the source location. But this requires more complex FSMs.

**Another new idea:** What if I try GoToColor targeting LED colors set by other robots? Robots in return state (S1) emit yellow (LED=4). Robots in foraging state emit nothing. Then foraging robots could use C=7 (perceives color 4=yellow) to follow returning robots back toward source? Wait - that would make them follow robots going TO the nest, not away from it.

**Actually:** Robots emitting yellow while AT the source could beacon others to the source. But the LED is set by state, not by floor color directly.

**Simplest reliable approach:** Keep the core 2-state design with single transitions. Try all combinations systematically. Also add some with intermediate exploration states.

**Important new variation:** What if the return uses GoAwayColor from the source color instead of GoToColor RED? This might be more robust:
- S1: GoAwayColor BLUE (clr=3) → moves away from blue = toward nest

This is the final round. I need to make my best possible machines.

**After 9 rounds of exploration, let me consolidate what I know:**

The theoretically correct design for this foraging task is:
- **Foraging state**: GoToColor [GOOD_SOURCE_COLOR] → when BLACK floor detected → switch to return state
- **Return state**: GoToColor RED (clr=1) → when WHITE floor detected → switch to foraging state

The two candidate good source colors are BLUE (clr=3) and GREEN (clr=2). I've been trying both equally.

**What I should maximize in the final round:**
1. Keep the cleanest versions of both BLUE-targeting and GREEN-targeting machines (elitism)

3. Try 3-state with exploration buffer
4. Make sure transition probabilities are exactly 1.0 for deterministic triggering
5. Single-transition states (most reliable)

**The absolute best candidate machines theoretically:**
- 2-state: GoToColor→BLACK→GoToColor RED→WHITE→repeat (with single transitions, p=1.0)
- 3-state: Same but with exploration buffer state for when robots get stuck

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
